Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.

It’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.

It happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.

Or if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.

description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: This has been especially noticeable for about 1 year and 10 months, since I took on the extra community center coordinating along with all my other activities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.

I’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.

In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.

I suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.

Like at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.

Or even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something.   },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.

Sometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.

For example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.

It happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.

So, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation), in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your constant activity and tendency to interrupt others affects your relationships with family and friends?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started causing real strain at home and at the community center, because my husband and other volunteers can’t count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it does, more than I used to admit. At home especially, my husband gets worn out with having to check behind me. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.

And it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.

So yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and tendency to interrupt others affects your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, more than I used to admit. At home especially, my husband gets worn out with having to check behind me. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.\n\nAnd it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.\n\nSo yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  {

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation). You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S014\n  name: Constant_Activity\n  description: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n  manifestation: I feel like I’m always up and moving, like even on a quiet day I’m bouncing from the garden club list to my knitting bag to travel papers before I’ve really finished any one thing.\n- key: S001\n  name: Detail_Inattention_Errors\n  description: Often fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes.\n  manifestation: When I help with community center papers, I’ll miss a name on the sign-up sheet or put the wrong room time on a note even though I thought I checked it.\n- key: S002\n  name: Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\n  manifestation: I can start out listening at a planning meeting or reading the church bulletin, but after a little while I realize I’ve missed half of what was said or printed.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: I have piles for dance class, knitting, garden events, and trips all over the kitchen table, and I can’t always figure out what needs to be done first.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: If someone’s talking in the next room or a car goes by the window, my mind jumps right over there and I lose what I was doing.\n- key: S009\n  name: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\n  description: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ll mean to call someone back, pick up something for supper, or write down an appointment, and then it just slips away until my husband asks about it.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: When I’m sitting in a meeting or even watching television, I’m usually tapping my foot, rubbing my fingers together, or fussing with whatever is in my lap.\n- key: S012\n  name: Feeling_Restless\n  description: Often runs about or climbs in situations where it is inappropriate. (Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: If I have to sit still for too long, I get this stirred-up feeling inside like I need to stand up and straighten something or go check on something.\n- key: S013\n  name: Quiet_Activity_Difficulty\n  description: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n  manifestation: Quiet things are hard for me to keep quiet, because even at knitting I end up chatting, humming, or getting up to look for supplies instead of just sitting calmly.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting in line at the pharmacy or waiting my turn to speak at a meeting makes me fidgety, and I catch myself sighing or jumping in too soon.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: My husband says I cut him off before he finishes, and I do the same in group planning because I’m afraid I’ll lose the thought if I don’t say it right then.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even back in grade school, my teachers told my mother I was bright but scattered, always losing papers, talking out of turn, and wiggling in my seat before I finished my work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started causing real strain at home and at the community center, because my husband and other volunteers can’t count on me to keep plans straight without someone checking behind me.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 10 months\n  manifestation: This has been especially noticeable for about 1 year and 10 months, since I took on the extra community center coordinating along with all my other activities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and tendency to interrupt others affects your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, more than I used to admit. At home especially, my husband gets worn out with having to check behind me. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.\n\nAnd it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.\n\nSo yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your constant activity and impulsivity are causing you significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 11\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

{"key":"functional_impairment","name":"Functional Impairment","description":"required","reason":"The question asks whether symptoms are causing significant problems in daily life or relationships, which directly maps to functional impairment."}

He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think it is causing real problems, even if they’re not dramatic problems, if that makes sense. It’s more this steady strain that builds up.

At home, my husband has gotten to where he feels like he has to check behind me. If there’s a plan we’ve talked about, or something I’m supposed to keep straight, he doesn’t really trust that I’ll hold onto all the pieces of it without something getting missed or muddled. And I can’t entirely blame him, but it doesn’t feel good. It makes me feel like I’m being watched or corrected, and then he gets frustrated, and I get embarrassed or defensive.

And at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind, but I can tell they don’t quite count on me the way they might count on someone else. If I’ve written something down or said I’d remember part of a plan, someone often checks it or follows up to make sure it’s right. Again, I understand why, but it’s still hard. It makes me feel less useful than I want to be.

So yes, it has started causing strain in my daily life and relationships. Not that everything is falling apart, but enough that other people are having to compensate for me, and I’m very aware of that.
