    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and tendency to interrupt others affects your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, more than I used to admit. At home especially, my husband gets worn out with having to check behind me. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.\n\nAnd it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.\n\nSo yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and impulsivity are causing you significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is causing real problems, even if they’re not dramatic problems, if that makes sense. It’s more this steady strain that builds up.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten to where he feels like he has to check behind me. If there’s a plan we’ve talked about, or something I’m supposed to keep straight, he doesn’t really trust that I’ll hold onto all the pieces of it without something getting missed or muddled. And I can’t entirely blame him, but it doesn’t feel good. It makes me feel like I’m being watched or corrected, and then he gets frustrated, and I get embarrassed or defensive.\n\nAnd at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind, but I can tell they don’t quite count on me the way they might count on someone else. If I’ve written something down or said I’d remember part of a plan, someone often checks it or follows up to make sure it’s right. Again, I understand why, but it’s still hard. It makes me feel less useful than I want to be.\n\nSo yes, it has started causing strain in my daily life and relationships. Not that everything is falling apart, but enough that other people are having to compensate for me, and I’m very aware of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness, impulsivity, and distraction since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t something that just started recently or even in adulthood. Even when I was in grade school, I remember my mother being told by teachers that I was bright, but scattered — that was the word they seemed to use, or at least that’s how it always got repeated at home.\n\nI was always losing papers, or not having the thing I was supposed to have, even if I had done the work. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not trying to be bad, exactly, but I’d have an answer or a thought and it would just come out before I waited my turn. I also remember wiggling around in my seat, especially if I hadn’t finished my work yet. I’d be half doing the assignment and half shifting around or looking for something.\n\nSo yes, when I think back on it, there were signs of this when I was a child. At the time I think people just thought I was careless or chatty, but it does feel like the same kind of pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that shows up a lot in my day-to-day life.\n\nI’ll have these piles on the kitchen table—one pile for my dance class things, another for knitting, then papers or notes for garden events, and then something else for a trip we’re taking or planning. In my mind, each pile is supposed to mean something, like I’m keeping things sorted, but then the whole table ends up covered and I stand there looking at it thinking, “All right, what am I supposed to do first?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the tasks. I do. Sometimes I care so much that I put everything where I can see it, because I’m afraid if I put it away I’ll forget it. But then seeing it all at once makes it harder to sort out what actually needs attention right now versus what can wait. So yes, organizing my time and tasks is definitely difficult for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “paying attention” at the time, of course, but looking back, that was part of it.\n\nEven in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright but scattered. That phrase stuck with me because it came up more than once. I’d lose papers or misplace things I was supposed to turn in, and sometimes I think I didn’t follow instructions all the way through—not because I was trying to ignore anyone, but because I’d miss a piece of what was said or get ahead of myself.\n\nI also talked out of turn a lot. If something popped into my head, it just came out before I really thought about waiting. And I remember wiggling in my seat, especially before I had finished my work. So yes, I think I did have trouble with attention and following through even as a child, though back then people mostly treated it like I was just careless or too talkative.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, leave a social group, or significantly change your daily routines because of your difficulty with restlessness, impulsivity, or distraction?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve exactly quit a job recently, since I’m not working now, but I have had to change how I do things because of it. It’s caused more strain than I like to admit, mostly at home and at the community center.\n\nMy husband has gotten to where he checks behind me on plans, because if we’re supposed to be somewhere or I said I’d bring something or call someone, he doesn’t always trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I don’t mean that in a mean way—he’s trying to help—but it feels awful, honestly. Like I’m a grown woman and still someone has to double-check me.\n\nAt the community center, it’s similar. I help with things there, and the other volunteers are kind, but I can tell they’ve learned not to assume that if I said I’d remember a detail, it’s settled. Someone will remind me, or they’ll repeat the plan, or sort of quietly make sure I understood what time or what task. So I haven’t necessarily left the group, but it has changed the way I participate. I feel like people can’t count on me unless someone is checking behind me, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to you being fired from a job or having significant problems at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I was ever fired for it. I’m not working now, so it’s a little hard to answer in the work sense.\n\nBut the closest thing I can say is that it has caused real strain in the places where people are counting on me. At home, my husband checks behind me with plans—what time we’re supposed to be somewhere, whether I said I’d bring something, things like that. And at the community center, the other volunteers are kind about it, but they’ve learned they can’t always just take my word that I’ve kept the details straight unless someone reminds me or double-checks.\n\nSo not fired, no. But there are definitely problems with people feeling like they can’t fully count on me unless there’s some backup, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still and your tendency to interrupt others are equally problematic for you, or does one cause you more trouble than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the interrupting causes me more trouble, honestly. The sitting still is uncomfortable and people may notice me shifting around, but the interrupting affects other people more directly.\n\nMy husband tells me I cut him off before he’s finished talking. And he’s right, I do it. I don’t mean to be rude, but if a thought comes into my head, I feel this pressure to say it right then because I’m afraid I’ll lose it. So I jump in, and then afterward I realize he wasn’t done. Sometimes he’ll stop and say, “Let me finish,” and I feel bad because I know I’ve done it again.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at the community center. If we’re talking through who’s doing what, I’ll suddenly think of something—like a question or a detail—and I blurt it out before there’s really a pause. I wasn’t trying to be a bad kid, but I’d just blurt things out before I was supposed to. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.\n\nAnd it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.\n\nSo yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and impulsivity are causing you significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is causing real problems, even if they’re not dramatic problems, if that makes sense. It’s more this steady strain that builds up.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten to where he feels like he has to check behind me. If there’s a plan we’ve talked about, or something I’m supposed to keep straight, he doesn’t really trust that I’ll hold onto all the pieces of it without something getting missed or muddled. And I can’t entirely blame him, but it doesn’t feel good. It makes me feel like I’m being watched or corrected, and then he gets frustrated, and I get embarrassed or defensive.\n\nAnd at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind, but I can tell they don’t quite count on me the way they might count on someone else. If I’ve written something down or said I’d remember part of a plan, someone often checks it or follows up to make sure it’s right. Again, I understand why, but it’s still hard. It makes me feel less useful than I want to be.\n\nSo yes, it has started causing strain in my daily life and relationships. Not that everything is falling apart, but enough that other people are having to compensate for me, and I’m very aware of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness, impulsivity, and distraction since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t something that just started recently or even in adulthood. Even when I was in grade school, I remember my mother being told by teachers that I was bright, but scattered — that was the word they seemed to use, or at least that’s how it always got repeated at home.\n\nI was always losing papers, or not having the thing I was supposed to have, even if I had done the work. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not trying to be bad, exactly, but I’d have an answer or a thought and it would just come out before I waited my turn. I also remember wiggling around in my seat, especially if I hadn’t finished my work yet. I’d be half doing the assignment and half shifting around or looking for something.\n\nSo yes, when I think back on it, there were signs of this when I was a child. At the time I think people just thought I was careless or chatty, but it does feel like the same kind of pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that shows up a lot in my day-to-day life.\n\nI’ll have these piles on the kitchen table—one pile for my dance class things, another for knitting, then papers or notes for garden events, and then something else for a trip we’re taking or planning. In my mind, each pile is supposed to mean something, like I’m keeping things sorted, but then the whole table ends up covered and I stand there looking at it thinking, “All right, what am I supposed to do first?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the tasks. I do. Sometimes I care so much that I put everything where I can see it, because I’m afraid if I put it away I’ll forget it. But then seeing it all at once makes it harder to sort out what actually needs attention right now versus what can wait. So yes, organizing my time and tasks is definitely difficult for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “paying attention” at the time, of course, but looking back, that was part of it.\n\nEven in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright but scattered. That phrase stuck with me because it came up more than once. I’d lose papers or misplace things I was supposed to turn in, and sometimes I think I didn’t follow instructions all the way through—not because I was trying to ignore anyone, but because I’d miss a piece of what was said or get ahead of myself.\n\nI also talked out of turn a lot. If something popped into my head, it just came out before I really thought about waiting. And I remember wiggling in my seat, especially before I had finished my work. So yes, I think I did have trouble with attention and following through even as a child, though back then people mostly treated it like I was just careless or too talkative.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, leave a social group, or significantly change your daily routines because of your difficulty with restlessness, impulsivity, or distraction?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve exactly quit a job recently, since I’m not working now, but I have had to change how I do things because of it. It’s caused more strain than I like to admit, mostly at home and at the community center.\n\nMy husband has gotten to where he checks behind me on plans, because if we’re supposed to be somewhere or I said I’d bring something or call someone, he doesn’t always trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I don’t mean that in a mean way—he’s trying to help—but it feels awful, honestly. Like I’m a grown woman and still someone has to double-check me.\n\nAt the community center, it’s similar. I help with things there, and the other volunteers are kind, but I can tell they’ve learned not to assume that if I said I’d remember a detail, it’s settled. Someone will remind me, or they’ll repeat the plan, or sort of quietly make sure I understood what time or what task. So I haven’t necessarily left the group, but it has changed the way I participate. I feel like people can’t count on me unless someone is checking behind me, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to you being fired from a job or having significant problems at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I was ever fired for it. I’m not working now, so it’s a little hard to answer in the work sense.\n\nBut the closest thing I can say is that it has caused real strain in the places where people are counting on me. At home, my husband checks behind me with plans—what time we’re supposed to be somewhere, whether I said I’d bring something, things like that. And at the community center, the other volunteers are kind about it, but they’ve learned they can’t always just take my word that I’ve kept the details straight unless someone reminds me or double-checks.\n\nSo not fired, no. But there are definitely problems with people feeling like they can’t fully count on me unless there’s some backup, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still and your tendency to interrupt others are equally problematic for you, or does one cause you more trouble than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the interrupting causes me more trouble, honestly. The sitting still is uncomfortable and people may notice me shifting around, but the interrupting affects other people more directly.\n\nMy husband tells me I cut him off before he’s finished talking. And he’s right, I do it. I don’t mean to be rude, but if a thought comes into my head, I feel this pressure to say it right then because I’m afraid I’ll lose it. So I jump in, and then afterward I realize he wasn’t done. Sometimes he’ll stop and say, “Let me finish,” and I feel bad because I know I’ve done it again.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at the community center. If we’re talking through who’s doing what, I’ll suddenly think of something—like a question or a detail—and I blurt it out before there’s really a pause. In my mind it feels urgent, like if I wait my turn, it’ll disappear. But I can see how it comes across as me intruding or not listening. So yes, I’d say the interrupting is the bigger problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. I remember my mother being told more than once by teachers that I was bright, but… scattered, I think was the kind of word they used. Like I could understand the work, but I’d lose the paper before turning it in, or forget where I put things, or not finish before I was already wiggling around in my seat.\n\nAnd talking out of turn, that was a big one too. I wasn’t trying to be a bad kid, but I’d just blurt things out before I was supposed to. My mother would come home from those teacher conferences sort of frustrated, saying, “They say you’re smart, so why can’t you just keep yourself together?” That’s how it felt, anyway. So yes, even back in grade school, it was something people noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do think it shows up in more than one place. It’s not just, say, something that happens at home with my husband because he knows me so well, or only at the community center because there are lots of details to keep track of. It’s kind of the same pattern in both places.

At home, my husband has gotten into the habit of checking behind me about plans—what time we’re leaving, whether I remembered what I said I would do, whether I’ve mixed up a detail. And I don’t like that, because it makes me feel like a child sometimes, but I also can’t honestly say he’s wrong to do it.

Then at the community center, it’s similar. The other volunteers are nice about it, but I can tell they don’t always fully trust that I’ve kept the plan straight unless someone reminds me or confirms it. So yes, across situations, it has been pretty consistent, and it’s started causing real strain because people feel like they can’t count on me without double-checking.

  {

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and tendency to interrupt others affects your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, more than I used to admit. At home especially, my husband gets worn out with having to check behind me. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.\n\nAnd it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.\n\nSo yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and impulsivity are causing you significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is causing real problems, even if they’re not dramatic problems, if that makes sense. It’s more this steady strain that builds up.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten to where he feels like he has to check behind me. If there’s a plan we’ve talked about, or something I’m supposed to keep straight, he doesn’t really trust that I’ll hold onto all the pieces of it without something getting missed or muddled. And I can’t entirely blame him, but it doesn’t feel good. It makes me feel like I’m being watched or corrected, and then he gets frustrated, and I get embarrassed or defensive.\n\nAnd at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind, but I can tell they don’t quite count on me the way they might count on someone else. If I’ve written something down or said I’d remember part of a plan, someone often checks it or follows up to make sure it’s right. Again, I understand why, but it’s still hard. It makes me feel less useful than I want to be.\n\nSo yes, it has started causing strain in my daily life and relationships. Not that everything is falling apart, but enough that other people are having to compensate for me, and I’m very aware of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness, impulsivity, and distraction since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t something that just started recently or even in adulthood. Even when I was in grade school, I remember my mother being told by teachers that I was bright, but scattered — that was the word they seemed to use, or at least that’s how it always got repeated at home.\n\nI was always losing papers, or not having the thing I was supposed to have, even if I had done the work. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not trying to be bad, exactly, but I’d have an answer or a thought and it would just come out before I waited my turn. I also remember wiggling around in my seat, especially if I hadn’t finished my work yet. I’d be half doing the assignment and half shifting around or looking for something.\n\nSo yes, when I think back on it, there were signs of this when I was a child. At the time I think people just thought I was careless or chatty, but it does feel like the same kind of pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that shows up a lot in my day-to-day life.\n\nI’ll have these piles on the kitchen table—one pile for my dance class things, another for knitting, then papers or notes for garden events, and then something else for a trip we’re taking or planning. In my mind, each pile is supposed to mean something, like I’m keeping things sorted, but then the whole table ends up covered and I stand there looking at it thinking, “All right, what am I supposed to do first?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the tasks. I do. Sometimes I care so much that I put everything where I can see it, because I’m afraid if I put it away I’ll forget it. But then seeing it all at once makes it harder to sort out what actually needs attention right now versus what can wait. So yes, organizing my time and tasks is definitely difficult for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “paying attention” at the time, of course, but looking back, that was part of it.\n\nEven in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright but scattered. That phrase stuck with me because it came up more than once. I’d lose papers or misplace things I was supposed to turn in, and sometimes I think I didn’t follow instructions all the way through—not because I was trying to ignore anyone, but because I’d miss a piece of what was said or get ahead of myself.\n\nI also talked out of turn a lot. If something popped into my head, it just came out before I really thought about waiting. And I remember wiggling in my seat, especially before I had finished my work. So yes, I think I did have trouble with attention and following through even as a child, though back then people mostly treated it like I was just careless or too talkative.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, leave a social group, or significantly change your daily routines because of your difficulty with restlessness, impulsivity, or distraction?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve exactly quit a job recently, since I’m not working now, but I have had to change how I do things because of it. It’s caused more strain than I like to admit, mostly at home and at the community center.\n\nMy husband has gotten to where he checks behind me on plans, because if we’re supposed to be somewhere or I said I’d bring something or call someone, he doesn’t always trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I don’t mean that in a mean way—he’s trying to help—but it feels awful, honestly. Like I’m a grown woman and still someone has to double-check me.\n\nAt the community center, it’s similar. I help with things there, and the other volunteers are kind, but I can tell they’ve learned not to assume that if I said I’d remember a detail, it’s settled. Someone will remind me, or they’ll repeat the plan, or sort of quietly make sure I understood what time or what task. So I haven’t necessarily left the group, but it has changed the way I participate. I feel like people can’t count on me unless someone is checking behind me, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to you being fired from a job or having significant problems at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I was ever fired for it. I’m not working now, so it’s a little hard to answer in the work sense.\n\nBut the closest thing I can say is that it has caused real strain in the places where people are counting on me. At home, my husband checks behind me with plans—what time we’re supposed to be somewhere, whether I said I’d bring something, things like that. And at the community center, the other volunteers are kind about it, but they’ve learned they can’t always just take my word that I’ve kept the details straight unless someone reminds me or double-checks.\n\nSo not fired, no. But there are definitely problems with people feeling like they can’t fully count on me unless there’s some backup, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still and your tendency to interrupt others are equally problematic for you, or does one cause you more trouble than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the interrupting causes me more trouble, honestly. The sitting still is uncomfortable and people may notice me shifting around, but the interrupting affects other people more directly.\n\nMy husband tells me I cut him off before he’s finished talking. And he’s right, I do it. I don’t mean to be rude, but if a thought comes into my head, I feel this pressure to say it right then because I’m afraid I’ll lose it. So I jump in, and then afterward I realize he wasn’t done. Sometimes he’ll stop and say, “Let me finish,” and I feel bad because I know I’ve done it again.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at the community center. If we’re talking through who’s doing what, I’ll suddenly think of something—like a question or a detail—and I blurt it out before there’s really a pause. In my mind it feels urgent, like if I wait my turn, it’ll disappear. But I can see how it comes across as me intruding or not listening. So yes, I’d say the interrupting is the bigger problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. I remember my mother being told more than once by teachers that I was bright, but… scattered, I think was the kind of word they used. Like I could understand the work, but I’d lose the paper before turning it in, or forget where I put things, or not finish before I was already wiggling around in my seat.\n\nAnd talking out of turn, that was a big one too. I wasn’t trying to be a bad kid, but I’d just blurt things out before I was supposed to. My mother would come home from those teacher conferences sort of frustrated, saying, “They say you’re smart, so why can’t you just keep yourself together?” That’s how it felt, anyway. So yes, even back in grade school, it was something people noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present and relatively consistent across different settings and situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it shows up in more than one place. It’s not just, say, something that happens at home with my husband because he knows me so well, or only at the community center because there are lots of details to keep track of. It’s kind of the same pattern in both places.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten into the habit of checking behind me about plans—what time we’re leaving, whether I remembered what I said I would do, whether I’ve mixed up a detail. And I don’t like that, because it makes me feel like a child sometimes, but I also can’t honestly say he’s wrong to do it.\n\nThen at the community center, it’s similar. The other volunteers are nice about it, but I can tell they don’t always fully trust that I’ve kept the plan straight unless someone reminds me or confirms it. So yes, across situations, it has been pretty consistent, and it’s started causing real strain because people feel like they can’t count on me without double-checking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not working now, so it’s a little hard to answer in the work sense.\n\nBut the closest thing I can say is that it has caused real strain in the places where people are counting on me. At home, my husband checks behind me with plans—what time we’re supposed to be somewhere, whether I said I’d bring something, things like that. And at the community center, the other volunteers are kind about it, but they’ve learned they can’t always just take my word that I’ve kept the details straight unless someone reminds me or double-checks.\n\nSo not fired, no. But there are definitely problems with people feeling like they can’t fully count on me unless there’s some backup, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still and your tendency to interrupt others are equally problematic for you, or does one cause you more trouble than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the interrupting causes me more trouble, honestly. The sitting still is uncomfortable and people may notice me shifting around, but the interrupting affects other people more directly.\n\nMy husband tells me I cut him off before he’s finished talking. And he’s right, I do it. I don’t mean to be rude, but if a thought comes into my head, I feel this pressure to say it right then because I’m afraid I’ll lose it. So I jump in, and then afterward I realize he wasn’t done. Sometimes he’ll stop and say, “Let me finish,” and I feel bad because I know I’ve done it again.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at the community center. If we’re talking through who’s doing what, I’ll suddenly think of something—like a question or a detail—and I blurt it out before there’s really a pause. In my mind it feels urgent, like if I wait my turn, it’ll disappear. But I can see how it comes across as me intruding or not listening. So yes, I’d say the interrupting is the bigger problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. I remember my mother being told more than once by teachers that I was bright, but… scattered, I think was the kind of word they used. Like I could understand the work, but I’d lose the paper before turning it in, or forget where I put things, or not finish before I was already wiggling around in my seat.\n\nAnd talking out of turn, that was a big one too. I wasn’t trying to be a bad kid, but I’d just blurt things out before I was supposed to. My mother would come home from those teacher conferences sort of frustrated, saying, “They say you’re smart, so why can’t you just keep yourself together?” That’s how it felt, anyway. So yes, even back in grade school, it was something people noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present and relatively consistent across different settings and situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it shows up in more than one place. It’s not just, say, something that happens at home with my husband because he knows me so well, or only at the community center because there are lots of details to keep track of. It’s kind of the same pattern in both places.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten into the habit of checking behind me about plans—what time we’re leaving, whether I remembered what I said I would do, whether I’ve mixed up a detail. And I don’t like that, because it makes me feel like a child sometimes, but I also can’t honestly say he’s wrong to do it.\n\nThen at the community center, it’s similar. The other volunteers are nice about it, but I can tell they don’t always fully trust that I’ve kept the plan straight unless someone reminds me or confirms it. So yes, across situations, it has been pretty consistent, and it’s started causing real strain because people feel like they can’t count on me without double-checking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think it has, especially more recently. I don’t want to make it sound like my whole life is falling apart, because it’s not that extreme, but it has started to cause real strain.

At home, my husband feels like he has to check behind me about plans, and I can tell it wears on him. Like if we’ve agreed on what time we’re doing something or what I’m supposed to remember, he’ll double-check, not in a mean way, but because he doesn’t fully trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I understand why, but it still feels awful, like I’m being managed.

And at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind people, but I can sense that they don’t always count on me to have the plan right unless someone confirms it or reminds me. That’s embarrassing. I want to be reliable, and I try to be, but when people feel they have to check behind me, it affects the relationship. So yes, it does interfere with daily life and with how people relate to me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what brings me in is that I feel like I’m always going, if that makes sense. Even on a perfectly quiet day, when nothing urgent is happening, I’ll be up and moving around from one thing to another. I’ll start looking at the garden club list, then I notice my knitting bag and think I should sort that out, and then before I know it I’m looking through travel papers or moving something from one room to another, and I haven’t really finished the first thing I sat down to do.\n\nIt’s not that I’m running around wildly or anything, but there’s this constant activity in me, like I can’t quite just settle into one thing. It’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should come talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do, though maybe not in dramatic ways. The main thing my husband points out is that I interrupt him. He’ll be halfway through telling me something, and I already know—or I think I know—what I want to say back, and I’ll jump in before he’s finished. I don’t always mean to. In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. I’ll cut in because I’m afraid the point I thought of will be lost if I wait for the right pause. Then afterward I realize I probably came across as rude or pushy, and I feel bad about it. So yes, I suppose that is acting before I’ve fully thought through how it’s going to land.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much so. It doesn’t take much, honestly. If I’m trying to read something or sort through papers, and I hear someone talking in the next room, even if I can’t make out the words, my mind sort of jumps over there. I’ll start wondering what they’re saying, or whether they’re talking to me, and then I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing.\n\nOr if a car goes by the window, especially if it’s a little loud or slow, I’ll look up, and then I’m thinking about who that was or whether someone’s arrived, and by the time I look back down, I have to start over. It’s frustrating because it feels so small—just a sound or something moving past—but it pulls me away before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Not always in a big obvious way, like bouncing out of the chair, but if I have to sit still for too long I get this sort of stirred-up feeling inside. It’s hard to explain—it’s like my body is sitting there, but the inside of me is already up and moving.\n\nI’ll start thinking, oh, I should just stand up and straighten that stack of papers, or maybe I should go check whether I left something in the other room, or I’ll notice something slightly out of place and feel like I need to get up and fix it right then. Even if there’s no real reason. Sitting through something long, I can feel myself getting more and more uncomfortable, like I’m waiting for a chance to move. So yes, I’d say I do get restless when I’m expected to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant feelings of anxiety or guilt?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “significant,” no. I mean, I can feel bad about things sometimes, like if I realize I’ve interrupted someone or I’ve made something more difficult than it needed to be, but I don’t know if I’d call that anxiety or guilt in a big way.\n\nI suppose I do second-guess myself here and there, but it doesn’t feel like the main thing I’m struggling with. It’s more that I get frustrated with myself afterward, if that makes sense. Like, “Why did I do that again?” But not, um… not constant guilt hanging over me or anything like that. And anxiety—well, I’d want to know what you mean exactly. I don’t think of myself as an especially anxious person, at least not in a way that feels overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to complete tasks or projects because you lose focus or get sidetracked?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though it’s not always that I completely abandon something. It’s more that I start out meaning to follow along, and then somewhere in the middle I sort of drift off without noticing.\n\nLike at a planning meeting at church, I’ll be listening at first, and I’ll think I’m taking it in, but then a little while later I realize they’ve moved on and I’ve missed half of what was said. Then I’m sitting there trying to piece it together from the bits I did catch, and I feel embarrassed asking them to repeat it.\n\nOr even with the church bulletin, I’ll start reading it because I do want to know what’s going on, and then I get to the bottom of the page and realize I don’t really know what I just read. So I have to go back over it again. It makes simple things take longer than they should, and I suppose that does make it harder to finish things properly.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty managing your emotions?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t say I have big emotional outbursts or anything like that, at least not that I think of. I mean, I can get frustrated with myself, especially if I’ve done something I didn’t mean to do or I can’t quite keep up with something, but I don’t know if that counts as difficulty managing emotions.\n\nSometimes I might feel irritated or embarrassed and it lingers a little, but I’m not, you know, losing control or having dramatic mood swings. At least I don’t think so. I suppose other people might see it differently, but from my side it feels more like ordinary frustration than something I’d call a serious problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities?\n\nPatient: Not really running or climbing, no—not at my age, certainly. But I do get a sort of restless feeling if I have to sit still too long. It’s hard to describe. It’s not like I’m thinking, “I need to go exercise,” exactly. It’s more this stirred-up feeling inside, like I should get up and do something.\n\nFor example, if I’m sitting for a long stretch, after a while I start feeling like I need to stand up and straighten something, or go check on something in another room. Sometimes it’s just little things—moving around a bit, fussing with something, making sure something is where it should be. I don’t know if that counts as needing intense activity, because it isn’t intense, really. It’s more like I don’t settle well when I’m expected to just sit there and stay put for too long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself talking excessively or dominating conversations?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say I “dominate” conversations exactly, but my husband would probably say I interrupt more than I realize. He’ll be telling me something, and I’ll jump in before he’s quite finished, not because I mean to be rude, but because a thought comes into my head and I feel like if I don’t say it right then, it’ll be gone.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at church meetings. Someone will be talking through an idea, and I’ll suddenly think of a question or a detail we need to remember, and I’ll put it in before they’ve finished. Then afterward I think, oh, I should have waited. But in the moment it feels urgent, like I have to get it out before I lose track of it.\n\nSo, talking excessively maybe not all the time, but cutting in, yes, I think I probably do that more than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and tendency to interrupt others affects your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, more than I used to admit. At home especially, my husband gets worn out with having to check behind me. If we’ve talked about a plan—like when we’re leaving, what we’re supposed to bring, who I said I’d call—I may think I’ve got it straight, but then I’ll miss a piece of it or mix something up, and he feels like he can’t just trust that I’ve kept it all in order.\n\nAnd it’s similar at the community center with the other volunteers. I don’t mean to make extra work for anyone, but if there are plans or sign-ups or little details to keep track of, people have started double-checking what I’ve written down or what I said I’d do. That’s embarrassing, honestly. It makes me feel like I’m being treated like I can’t be relied on, but at the same time I can see why they do it.\n\nSo yes, it has caused some strain. Not that people are cruel about it, but there’s this tension—my husband gets frustrated, and the volunteers can’t really count on me without someone making sure the plan is still straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant activity and impulsivity are causing you significant problems in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think it is causing real problems, even if they’re not dramatic problems, if that makes sense. It’s more this steady strain that builds up.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten to where he feels like he has to check behind me. If there’s a plan we’ve talked about, or something I’m supposed to keep straight, he doesn’t really trust that I’ll hold onto all the pieces of it without something getting missed or muddled. And I can’t entirely blame him, but it doesn’t feel good. It makes me feel like I’m being watched or corrected, and then he gets frustrated, and I get embarrassed or defensive.\n\nAnd at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind, but I can tell they don’t quite count on me the way they might count on someone else. If I’ve written something down or said I’d remember part of a plan, someone often checks it or follows up to make sure it’s right. Again, I understand why, but it’s still hard. It makes me feel less useful than I want to be.\n\nSo yes, it has started causing strain in my daily life and relationships. Not that everything is falling apart, but enough that other people are having to compensate for me, and I’m very aware of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of restlessness, impulsivity, and distraction since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. Looking back, it wasn’t something that just started recently or even in adulthood. Even when I was in grade school, I remember my mother being told by teachers that I was bright, but scattered — that was the word they seemed to use, or at least that’s how it always got repeated at home.\n\nI was always losing papers, or not having the thing I was supposed to have, even if I had done the work. And I talked out of turn a lot. Not trying to be bad, exactly, but I’d have an answer or a thought and it would just come out before I waited my turn. I also remember wiggling around in my seat, especially if I hadn’t finished my work yet. I’d be half doing the assignment and half shifting around or looking for something.\n\nSo yes, when I think back on it, there were signs of this when I was a child. At the time I think people just thought I was careless or chatty, but it does feel like the same kind of pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing your time or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the things that shows up a lot in my day-to-day life.\n\nI’ll have these piles on the kitchen table—one pile for my dance class things, another for knitting, then papers or notes for garden events, and then something else for a trip we’re taking or planning. In my mind, each pile is supposed to mean something, like I’m keeping things sorted, but then the whole table ends up covered and I stand there looking at it thinking, “All right, what am I supposed to do first?”\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care about the tasks. I do. Sometimes I care so much that I put everything where I can see it, because I’m afraid if I put it away I’ll forget it. But then seeing it all at once makes it harder to sort out what actually needs attention right now versus what can wait. So yes, organizing my time and tasks is definitely difficult for me.\n\nDoctor: Did you have difficulty paying attention or following instructions when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I did. I mean, I wouldn’t have called it “paying attention” at the time, of course, but looking back, that was part of it.\n\nEven in grade school, my teachers used to tell my mother that I was bright but scattered. That phrase stuck with me because it came up more than once. I’d lose papers or misplace things I was supposed to turn in, and sometimes I think I didn’t follow instructions all the way through—not because I was trying to ignore anyone, but because I’d miss a piece of what was said or get ahead of myself.\n\nI also talked out of turn a lot. If something popped into my head, it just came out before I really thought about waiting. And I remember wiggling in my seat, especially before I had finished my work. So yes, I think I did have trouble with attention and following through even as a child, though back then people mostly treated it like I was just careless or too talkative.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to quit a job, leave a social group, or significantly change your daily routines because of your difficulty with restlessness, impulsivity, or distraction?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve exactly quit a job recently, since I’m not working now, but I have had to change how I do things because of it. It’s caused more strain than I like to admit, mostly at home and at the community center.\n\nMy husband has gotten to where he checks behind me on plans, because if we’re supposed to be somewhere or I said I’d bring something or call someone, he doesn’t always trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I don’t mean that in a mean way—he’s trying to help—but it feels awful, honestly. Like I’m a grown woman and still someone has to double-check me.\n\nAt the community center, it’s similar. I help with things there, and the other volunteers are kind, but I can tell they’ve learned not to assume that if I said I’d remember a detail, it’s settled. Someone will remind me, or they’ll repeat the plan, or sort of quietly make sure I understood what time or what task. So I haven’t necessarily left the group, but it has changed the way I participate. I feel like people can’t count on me unless someone is checking behind me, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever led to you being fired from a job or having significant problems at work?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I was ever fired for it. I’m not working now, so it’s a little hard to answer in the work sense.\n\nBut the closest thing I can say is that it has caused real strain in the places where people are counting on me. At home, my husband checks behind me with plans—what time we’re supposed to be somewhere, whether I said I’d bring something, things like that. And at the community center, the other volunteers are kind about it, but they’ve learned they can’t always just take my word that I’ve kept the details straight unless someone reminds me or double-checks.\n\nSo not fired, no. But there are definitely problems with people feeling like they can’t fully count on me unless there’s some backup, and that’s embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still and your tendency to interrupt others are equally problematic for you, or does one cause you more trouble than the other?\n\nPatient: I think the interrupting causes me more trouble, honestly. The sitting still is uncomfortable and people may notice me shifting around, but the interrupting affects other people more directly.\n\nMy husband tells me I cut him off before he’s finished talking. And he’s right, I do it. I don’t mean to be rude, but if a thought comes into my head, I feel this pressure to say it right then because I’m afraid I’ll lose it. So I jump in, and then afterward I realize he wasn’t done. Sometimes he’ll stop and say, “Let me finish,” and I feel bad because I know I’ve done it again.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like at the community center. If we’re talking through who’s doing what, I’ll suddenly think of something—like a question or a detail—and I blurt it out before there’s really a pause. In my mind it feels urgent, like if I wait my turn, it’ll disappear. But I can see how it comes across as me intruding or not listening. So yes, I’d say the interrupting is the bigger problem for me.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. I remember my mother being told more than once by teachers that I was bright, but… scattered, I think was the kind of word they used. Like I could understand the work, but I’d lose the paper before turning it in, or forget where I put things, or not finish before I was already wiggling around in my seat.\n\nAnd talking out of turn, that was a big one too. I wasn’t trying to be a bad kid, but I’d just blurt things out before I was supposed to. My mother would come home from those teacher conferences sort of frustrated, saying, “They say you’re smart, so why can’t you just keep yourself together?” That’s how it felt, anyway. So yes, even back in grade school, it was something people noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present and relatively consistent across different settings and situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it shows up in more than one place. It’s not just, say, something that happens at home with my husband because he knows me so well, or only at the community center because there are lots of details to keep track of. It’s kind of the same pattern in both places.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten into the habit of checking behind me about plans—what time we’re leaving, whether I remembered what I said I would do, whether I’ve mixed up a detail. And I don’t like that, because it makes me feel like a child sometimes, but I also can’t honestly say he’s wrong to do it.\n\nThen at the community center, it’s similar. The other volunteers are nice about it, but I can tell they don’t always fully trust that I’ve kept the plan straight unless someone reminds me or confirms it. So yes, across situations, it has been pretty consistent, and it’s started causing real strain because people feel like they can’t count on me without double-checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, especially more recently. I don’t want to make it sound like my whole life is falling apart, because it’s not that extreme, but it has started to cause real strain.\n\nAt home, my husband feels like he has to check behind me about plans, and I can tell it wears on him. Like if we’ve agreed on what time we’re doing something or what I’m supposed to remember, he’ll double-check, not in a mean way, but because he doesn’t fully trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I understand why, but it still feels awful, like I’m being managed.\n\nAnd at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind people, but I can sense that they don’t always count on me to have the plan right unless someone confirms it or reminds me. That’s embarrassing. I want to be reliable, and I try to be, but when people feel they have to check behind me, it affects the relationship. So yes, it does interfere with daily life and with how people relate to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

In the moment it feels like if I don’t say it right then, the thought will just vanish, and then I’ll be sitting there trying to remember what it was.\n\nIt happens in group planning too, like when we’re talking through arrangements or deciding who’s doing what. My mother would come home from those teacher conferences sort of frustrated, saying, “They say you’re smart, so why can’t you just keep yourself together?” That’s how it felt, anyway. So yes, even back in grade school, it was something people noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with attention and impulsivity have been present and relatively consistent across different settings and situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do think it shows up in more than one place. It’s not just, say, something that happens at home with my husband because he knows me so well, or only at the community center because there are lots of details to keep track of. It’s kind of the same pattern in both places.\n\nAt home, my husband has gotten into the habit of checking behind me about plans—what time we’re leaving, whether I remembered what I said I would do, whether I’ve mixed up a detail. And I don’t like that, because it makes me feel like a child sometimes, but I also can’t honestly say he’s wrong to do it.\n\nThen at the community center, it’s similar. The other volunteers are nice about it, but I can tell they don’t always fully trust that I’ve kept the plan straight unless someone reminds me or confirms it. So yes, across situations, it has been pretty consistent, and it’s started causing real strain because people feel like they can’t count on me without double-checking.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your symptoms have significantly impacted your ability to function in daily life or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, especially more recently. I don’t want to make it sound like my whole life is falling apart, because it’s not that extreme, but it has started to cause real strain.\n\nAt home, my husband feels like he has to check behind me about plans, and I can tell it wears on him. Like if we’ve agreed on what time we’re doing something or what I’m supposed to remember, he’ll double-check, not in a mean way, but because he doesn’t fully trust that I’ve kept it straight. And I understand why, but it still feels awful, like I’m being managed.\n\nAnd at the community center, it’s similar with the other volunteers. They’re kind people, but I can sense that they don’t always count on me to have the plan right unless someone confirms it or reminds me. That’s embarrassing. I want to be reliable, and I try to be, but when people feel they have to check behind me, it affects the relationship. So yes, it does interfere with daily life and with how people relate to me."
